### Multi-population GAN Training: Analyzing Co-Evolutionary Algorithms
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.13157v1)
- **Authors**: Walter P. Casas, Jamal Toutouh
- **Abstract**: Generative adversarial networks (GANs) are powerful generative models but remain challenging to train due to pathologies suchas mode collapse and instability. Recent research has explored co-evolutionary approaches, in which populations of generators and discriminators are evolved, as a promising solution. This paper presents an empirical analysis of different coevolutionary GAN training strategies, focusing on the impact of selection and replacement mechanisms. We compare (mu,lambda), (mu+lambda) with elitism, and (mu+lambda) with tournament selection coevolutionary schemes, along with a non-evolutionary population based multi-generator multi-discriminator GAN baseline, across both synthetic low-dimensional datasets (blob and gaussian mixtures) and an image-based benchmark (MNIST). Results show that full generational replacement, i.e., (mu,lambda), consistently outperforms in terms of both sample quality and diversity, particularly when combined with larger offspring sizes. In contrast, elitist approaches tend to converge prematurely and suffer from reduced diversity. These findings highlight the importance of balancing exploration and exploitation dynamics in coevolutionary GAN training and provide guidance for designing more effective population-based generative models.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper based on the provided text:

**Summary:**

The paper "Multi-population GAN Training: Analyzing Co-Evolutionary Algorithms" investigates the impact of different co-evolutionary strategies on the training of Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs). The study focuses on how selection and replacement mechanisms within co-evolutionary algorithms influence the quality and diversity of generated samples. The paper compares (μ, λ), (μ+λ) with elitism, and (μ+λ) with tournament selection coevolutionary schemes, and an MG-MD (multi-generator multi-discriminator) GAN baseline.  The authors conduct experiments on synthetic datasets (blob and gaussian mixtures) and the MNIST dataset, evaluating the performance of the different co-evolutionary GAN training strategies in terms of sample quality and diversity, in order to see which strategy leads to the best balance between exploration and exploitation during training. The key finding is that full generational replacement (μ, λ) consistently outperforms other methods in terms of sample quality and diversity, particularly when combined with larger offspring sizes. Elitist approaches tend to converge prematurely and suffer from reduced diversity.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Systematic Analysis:** The paper provides a systematic and comparative analysis of different co-evolutionary GAN training strategies. This is valuable because it moves beyond simply proposing a new method to understanding the nuances of existing methods.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experiments are conducted on multiple datasets with appropriate metrics for assessing quality and diversity.
    *   **Clear Research Questions:** The research questions are clearly defined and drive the experimental design.
    *   **Insightful Findings:** The paper reveals important insights about the role of exploration and exploitation dynamics in co-evolutionary GAN training. Specifically, the finding that full generational replacement outperforms elitist approaches is significant and challenges the intuition that preserving top individuals is always beneficial.
    *   **Connections to Previous Work:** The paper effectively situates its contribution within the existing literature on GANs and co-evolutionary algorithms.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Dataset Scope:** While MNIST is a standard benchmark, and the synthetic datasets are useful for controlled experiments, the paper would be strengthened by including more complex and real-world datasets. The results may not fully generalize to more complex data distributions.
    *   **Architecture Limitations:** While different architectures tailored to the dataset are used, it is not clear how the selection of architectures was determined, or if a wider range of architectures could have influenced the results.
    *   **Lack of a Novel Algorithm:** The paper primarily *analyzes* existing co-evolutionary strategies rather than proposing a fundamentally new algorithm. This is not necessarily a weakness, but it affects the overall "novelty" score.
    *   **Hyperparameter Tuning:** While the paper mentions preliminary experiments to confirm hyper-parameters, more details on the tuning process would have been useful for reproducibility and understanding the sensitivity of results to these settings. The choices, given that each method has a different selection and replacement strategy, could be argued to influence the comparison.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    The paper's novelty lies primarily in its **thorough and comparative empirical analysis** of different co-evolutionary GAN training strategies. While the individual co-evolutionary strategies themselves may not be entirely new, the paper provides new insights into their strengths and weaknesses, particularly concerning the trade-off between exploration and exploitation. The finding that full generational replacement is more effective than elitist approaches is a valuable contribution that could guide future research in this area. The paper does a good job in providing clear analysis to the performance differences between full generational (exploration based) strategies vs elitist based (exploitation) ones.

    The significance of the paper lies in its potential to **improve the training and performance of GANs.** By understanding the impact of different co-evolutionary dynamics, researchers can design more effective population-based GAN training methods that are less prone to mode collapse and instability. This could lead to the development of more powerful generative models with applications in various fields, like better architectures for training GANs.

*   **Overall Assessment:**

    The paper is a solid contribution to the field of GAN training, characterized by its systematic analysis, empirical validation, and insightful findings. While the lack of a novel algorithm and the limited dataset scope are minor weaknesses, the paper's strengths outweigh these limitations. The paper is of interest to the evolutionary GAN community for its systematic overview and practical results.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The paper is a good, solid contribution to the field, but the lack of a completely novel algorithm and the limited dataset prevents it from being a higher impact work. The systematic analysis and practical results make it more than just a minor study, deserving of a 7.

- **Classification**: cs.NE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Inverse Reinforcement Learning Meets Large Language Model Post-Training: Basics, Advances, and Opportunities
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.13158v1)
- **Authors**: Hao Sun, Mihaela van der Schaar
- **Abstract**: In the era of Large Language Models (LLMs), alignment has emerged as a fundamental yet challenging problem in the pursuit of more reliable, controllable, and capable machine intelligence. The recent success of reasoning models and conversational AI systems has underscored the critical role of reinforcement learning (RL) in enhancing these systems, driving increased research interest at the intersection of RL and LLM alignment. This paper provides a comprehensive review of recent advances in LLM alignment through the lens of inverse reinforcement learning (IRL), emphasizing the distinctions between RL techniques employed in LLM alignment and those in conventional RL tasks. In particular, we highlight the necessity of constructing neural reward models from human data and discuss the formal and practical implications of this paradigm shift. We begin by introducing fundamental concepts in RL to provide a foundation for readers unfamiliar with the field. We then examine recent advances in this research agenda, discussing key challenges and opportunities in conducting IRL for LLM alignment. Beyond methodological considerations, we explore practical aspects, including datasets, benchmarks, evaluation metrics, infrastructure, and computationally efficient training and inference techniques. Finally, we draw insights from the literature on sparse-reward RL to identify open questions and potential research directions. By synthesizing findings from diverse studies, we aim to provide a structured and critical overview of the field, highlight unresolved challenges, and outline promising future directions for improving LLM alignment through RL and IRL techniques.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper abstract and introduction:

**Summary:**

This paper proposes a tutorial on Inverse Reinforcement Learning (IRL) applied to Large Language Model (LLM) alignment. The tutorial aims to bridge the gap between traditional RL and the specific challenges of aligning LLMs with human values and preferences. It highlights the shift from rule-based reward signals in conventional RL tasks to neural reward models derived from human data in the LLM context. The tutorial covers foundational RL concepts, recent advances in IRL for LLM alignment, practical considerations like datasets and efficient training, and open research questions inspired by sparse-reward RL literature.  The primary goal is to provide a structured overview, highlight challenges, and outline promising future directions in using RL and IRL techniques to improve LLM alignment. The paper emphasizes the importance of efficient reward modeling and addresses computational limitations by proposing a reward model infrastructure that reduces the reliance on GPUs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The novelty of this paper resides primarily in its framing and synthesis of existing work, rather than presenting entirely new algorithms or theoretical breakthroughs. While the individual components (RL, IRL, LLM alignment) are well-established, the paper's contribution is in:

*   **Synthesis of Existing Knowledge:** Consolidating various approaches in RL and IRL under the umbrella of LLM alignment, offering a structured overview of the field.
*   **Emphasis on Data-Driven Rewards:** Recognizing and highlighting the critical shift from rule-based rewards in classical RL to learning reward functions directly from human data (preferences, demonstrations) in the LLM alignment context.
*   **Identifying Open Research Questions:** Drawing parallels from sparse-reward RL to guide future research directions in LLM alignment, particularly in addressing challenges like reward overoptimization and distributional shift.
*   **Practical Focus:** The paper's emphasis on practical considerations, such as datasets, benchmarks, efficient training, and reducing reliance on computational resources (e.g., GPUs), makes it useful for researchers entering the field.

**Significance:** The significance of this work stems from the increasing importance of LLM alignment. The tutorial provides a framework for approaching this challenge using RL and IRL, which are promising techniques. The identification of challenges, such as reward hacking and the shift to learning rewards from human feedback, directly address key issues in deploying reliable and controllable LLMs. The practical focus on making the research more accessible to researchers with limited computational resources significantly widens the potential pool of contributors. The survey, challenges, and opportunities should provide a useful reference to the field.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Lack of Algorithmic Novelty:** It doesn't introduce fundamentally new RL or IRL algorithms tailored specifically for LLM alignment, but it provides a framework to consider existing RL tools and to evaluate which are effective.
*   **Limited Depth in Specific Areas:** Given its broad scope, the tutorial might lack in-depth analysis of specific RL or IRL algorithms. The depth may depend on the length of the submitted paper.
*   **Dependency on Future Research:** The "future directions" section is dependent on the success of future research.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper's strengths lie in its synthesis, practical focus, and the identification of crucial challenges and opportunities in the field. While it doesn't introduce groundbreaking theoretical contributions, its value as a comprehensive tutorial and guide is substantial.  It frames the problem in an accessible way, connects it to relevant literature, and addresses the practical concerns that often hinder research progress. The paper identifies challenges such as the lack of datasets, need for scalable methods for experimentation, as well as reward misspecification issues. The emphasis on making research in LLM alignment more accessible is a notable positive.

However, its relative lack of algorithmic novelty and dependency on future research directions warrants a more conservative scoring.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### SHIELD: A Secure and Highly Enhanced Integrated Learning for Robust Deepfake Detection against Adversarial Attacks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.13170v1)
- **Authors**: Kutub Uddin, Awais Khan, Muhammad Umar Farooq, Khalid Malik
- **Abstract**: Audio plays a crucial role in applications like speaker verification, voice-enabled smart devices, and audio conferencing. However, audio manipulations, such as deepfakes, pose significant risks by enabling the spread of misinformation. Our empirical analysis reveals that existing methods for detecting deepfake audio are often vulnerable to anti-forensic (AF) attacks, particularly those attacked using generative adversarial networks. In this article, we propose a novel collaborative learning method called SHIELD to defend against generative AF attacks. To expose AF signatures, we integrate an auxiliary generative model, called the defense (DF) generative model, which facilitates collaborative learning by combining input and output. Furthermore, we design a triplet model to capture correlations for real and AF attacked audios with real-generated and attacked-generated audios using auxiliary generative models. The proposed SHIELD strengthens the defense against generative AF attacks and achieves robust performance across various generative models. The proposed AF significantly reduces the average detection accuracy from 95.49% to 59.77% for ASVspoof2019, from 99.44% to 38.45% for In-the-Wild, and from 98.41% to 51.18% for HalfTruth for three different generative models. The proposed SHIELD mechanism is robust against AF attacks and achieves an average accuracy of 98.13%, 98.58%, and 99.57% in match, and 98.78%, 98.62%, and 98.85% in mismatch settings for the ASVspoof2019, In-the-Wild, and HalfTruth datasets, respectively.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "SHIELD: A Secure and Highly Enhanced Integrated Learning for Robust Deepfake Detection against Adversarial Attacks" proposes a novel collaborative learning method (SHIELD) to improve the robustness of audio deepfake detection systems against generative adversarial attacks. SHIELD integrates a defense generative model (DF) to expose adversarial signatures and uses a triplet model to capture correlations between real and attacked audio samples. The method aims to defend against anti-forensic attacks generated by generative adversarial networks (GANs), which significantly degrade the performance of existing audio deepfake detectors.  Experimental results on ASVspoof2019, In-the-Wild, and HalfTruth datasets demonstrate that SHIELD substantially reduces the impact of adversarial attacks and achieves high accuracy in both matched and mismatched attack scenarios.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using a defense generative model in a collaborative learning framework to expose adversarial signatures is relatively novel in the context of audio deepfake detection. The integration of a triplet model to capture relationships between real, generated, and attacked audio adds another layer of robustness.  While adversarial training and similar concepts exist, the specific architecture and application within the audio deepfake domain appears to be a valuable contribution. However, the use of GANs for anti-forensic attacks and triplet loss for discriminative learning, while not new in general machine learning, is still somewhat novel in this specific problem domain. The integration of a defense model to expose underlying artifacts left by the initial GAN attacks is a novel idea.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical vulnerability in audio deepfake detection systems – their susceptibility to adversarial attacks. Given the increasing prevalence of audio deepfakes and their potential for misuse, improving the security and robustness of detection methods is highly significant. The experimental results indicate that SHIELD is effective in mitigating the impact of GAN-based attacks, suggesting that it has practical value for real-world deployment. The datasets used are well known and this allows for a direct comparison with current methods. This provides strong support for the claims made in the paper. The SHIELD approach could have substantial impact, reducing the risk of successful deepfake attacks on various applications like smart devices, fraud detection, and misinformation campaigns.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clearly defined problem and motivation.
    *   Well-explained methodology with detailed architectural overview.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation on benchmark datasets.
    *   Demonstrated superior performance compared to existing defenses, especially against generative adversarial attacks.
    *   The writing is well-structured.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The paper could benefit from a more in-depth analysis of the computational overhead introduced by the added DF generative model and triplet loss. This would be an important factor for practical implementation, as the computational cost might make the methods harder to use.
    *   The experiments focus primarily on GAN-based adversarial attacks. While significant, it would be valuable to evaluate SHIELD's performance against a broader range of attack techniques (e.g., diffusion based approaches).
    * The triplet loss function is relatively simple. The paper would benefit from a discussion about potential improvements, such as the introduction of hard negative mining, which could lead to further performance gains.
    * The approach relies on a strong, accurate ADD as the final stage of their pipeline. If an advanced attack can significantly reduce ADD accuracy, the effectiveness of SHIELD may be limited. An in-depth ablation study analyzing the impact of ADD component is crucial to validate the claim

*   **Impact:** If the method proves robust in real-world deployment, it could significantly improve the reliability of audio deepfake detection and reduce the risks associated with audio manipulation. The idea of defense generative models could inspire other research in adversarial defense across different domains. The main contribution in this paper is to effectively bridge the gap in AF defense and generative attack, enabling more resilient and adaptive detection.

*   **Justification for Score:**
    The paper provides a solid contribution to the field of audio deepfake detection. It addresses an important vulnerability with a novel and effective method. The experimental results strongly support the claims made. However, the limited evaluation against a broader range of attacks, the lack of analysis about potential limitations, and the somewhat complex implementation (requiring a separate defensive GAN model) limit the overall impact. The integration of a defense model to expose artifacts left by the initial GAN attacks is a valuable idea.

Score: 7.5

### Black Box Deployed -- Functional Criteria for Artificial Moral Agents in the LLM Era
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.13175v1)
- **Authors**: Matthew E. Brophy
- **Abstract**: The advancement of powerful yet opaque large language models (LLMs) necessitates a fundamental revision of the philosophical criteria used to evaluate artificial moral agents (AMAs). Pre-LLM frameworks often relied on the assumption of transparent architectures, which LLMs defy due to their stochastic outputs and opaque internal states. This paper argues that traditional ethical criteria are pragmatically obsolete for LLMs due to this mismatch. Engaging with core themes in the philosophy of technology, this paper proffers a revised set of ten functional criteria to evaluate LLM-based artificial moral agents: moral concordance, context sensitivity, normative integrity, metaethical awareness, system resilience, trustworthiness, corrigibility, partial transparency, functional autonomy, and moral imagination. These guideposts, applied to what we term "SMA-LLS" (Simulating Moral Agency through Large Language Systems), aim to steer AMAs toward greater alignment and beneficial societal integration in the coming years. We illustrate these criteria using hypothetical scenarios involving an autonomous public bus (APB) to demonstrate their practical applicability in morally salient contexts.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, "Black Box Deployed: Functional Criteria for Artificial Moral Agents in the LLM Era" by Matthew E. Brophy:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenges of evaluating the moral behavior of AI systems, specifically large language models (LLMs), given their opaque "black box" nature. Traditional ethical frameworks, which often assume transparent architectures and rule-based operations, are inadequate for assessing LLMs due to their stochastic outputs and complex internal states. The paper proposes a revised set of ten functional criteria for evaluating LLM-based Artificial Moral Agents (AMAs): moral concordance, context sensitivity, normative integrity, metaethical awareness, system resilience, trustworthiness, corrigibility, partial transparency, functional autonomy, and moral imagination. These criteria shift the focus from internal understanding or intention to observable behavior and outputs. The paper introduces the concept of "SMA-LLS" (Simulating Moral Agency through Large Language Systems) and illustrates the application of these criteria through hypothetical scenarios involving an Autonomous Public Bus (APB).

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addresses a critical gap:** The paper directly tackles a pressing issue in AI ethics: the inadequacy of existing frameworks for evaluating LLMs.  This is increasingly relevant as LLMs are deployed in morally sensitive contexts.
    *   **Novel criteria:** The proposed functional criteria are well-defined and offer a practical approach to assessing LLMs without requiring access to their internal states. These criteria focus on observable behavior and demonstrable capacities, making them actionable.
    *   **Practical illustration:** The APB scenarios provide concrete examples of how the criteria can be applied in real-world situations, enhancing the paper's accessibility and practical relevance. The inclusion of supplementary material showing ChatGPT-4o's responses is valuable.
    *   **Philosophically informed:** The paper engages with relevant philosophical concepts like functionalism, Dennett's intentional stance, and ethical theories, providing a solid theoretical foundation for the proposed criteria.
    *   **Acknowledges limitations:** The author acknowledges limitations of the proposed criteria, including the difficulty of precise measurement and the challenges of implementing these criteria in practice. This self-awareness strengthens the paper's credibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Measurement challenges:** While the paper outlines approaches to measuring the criteria, many of them (especially moral imagination, systemic resilience, and metaethical awareness) remain difficult to operationalize and quantify rigorously. The metrics section (Section 7) feels somewhat aspirational.
    *   **Emphasis on externalism:**  The strong emphasis on externalism (observable behavior) might downplay the importance of exploring potential methods, however limited, for improving transparency into the reasoning processes of LLMs. While acknowledging the 'black box,' completely sidelining efforts towards explainability is debatable, particularly if future technologies allow even partial access to reasoning pathways.
    *   **Hypothetical scenarios:** The APB scenarios are illustrative, but they are still hypothetical. The real-world deployment of these criteria would require extensive empirical testing and validation. There's no mention of partnerships or future directions for testing, which might diminish the paper's long-term influence.
    *   **Limited Criticality in Scenario Responses:** The scenario responses from ChatGPT are presented without a high degree of criticality. While supplementary material shows that even older LLMs might be able to approximate the functional guidelines provided, a comparative analysis contrasting LLM responses vs. a baseline control of human evaluators might have made the case more compelling.
    *   **Potential for "Moral Washing":** Although the authors explicitly state the concerns with "moral fakery," relying solely on these metrics might incentivize superficial ethical performance without genuine integration of moral values.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**
    The paper's novelty lies in its shift from traditional, transparency-based ethical frameworks to a functional approach tailored specifically for LLMs. While the idea of functionalism in AI ethics isn't entirely new, the specific set of criteria and their application to LLMs in morally complex scenarios represents a significant contribution. The paper also addresses real-world and pragmatic concerns with these ethical frameworks and addresses edge cases that existing ethical guidelines might not be able to approach. This is important as LLMs get deployed in our societies, as the paper explicitly states. This novel approach is therefore significant. The paper's potential impact lies in its ability to guide the development and evaluation of more ethically aligned LLMs.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field of AI ethics by providing a practical and philosophically grounded framework for evaluating LLM-based AMAs. While challenges remain in measuring and implementing the proposed criteria, the paper offers a necessary and timely shift in perspective. Given the paper's strengths, while also acknowledging that the operationalization of some elements will present ongoing problems, a score of 7.0 is well-justified.

**Score: 7.0**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Enhancing Cross-task Transfer of Large Language Models via Activation Steering
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.13236v1)
- **Authors**: Xinyu Tang, Zhihao Lv, Xiaoxue Cheng, Junyi Li, Wayne Xin Zhao, Zujie Wen, Zhiqiang Zhang, Jun Zhou
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have shown impressive abilities in leveraging pretrained knowledge through prompting, but they often struggle with unseen tasks, particularly in data-scarce scenarios. While cross-task in-context learning offers a direct solution for transferring knowledge across tasks, it still faces critical challenges in terms of robustness, scalability, and efficiency. In this paper, we investigate whether cross-task transfer can be achieved via latent space steering without parameter updates or input expansion. Through an analysis of activation patterns in the latent space of LLMs, we observe that the enhanced activations induced by in-context examples have consistent patterns across different tasks. Inspired by these findings, we propose CAST, a novel Cross-task Activation Steering Transfer framework that enables effective transfer by manipulating the model's internal activation states. Our approach first selects influential and diverse samples from high-resource tasks, then utilizes their contrastive representation-enhanced activations to adapt LLMs to low-resource tasks. Extensive experiments across both cross-domain and cross-lingual transfer settings show that our method outperforms competitive baselines and demonstrates superior scalability and lower computational costs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of cross-task transfer in Large Language Models (LLMs), particularly in data-scarce scenarios. Instead of relying on parameter updates or input expansion, the authors propose a method called CAST (Cross-task Activation Steering Transfer) which operates by steering the latent space of LLMs. The key idea is that the activation differences between few-shot and zero-shot prompts exhibit consistent patterns across different tasks. CAST first selects influential and diverse samples from high-resource tasks. It then uses the contrastive representation-enhanced activations of these samples to adapt LLMs to low-resource tasks by manipulating the model's internal activation states. The paper demonstrates through experiments in cross-domain and cross-lingual settings that CAST outperforms competitive baselines while exhibiting scalability and lower computational costs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty stems from its approach to cross-task transfer. While in-context learning and soft prompt tuning are established techniques, the use of activation steering *without* parameter updates or input expansion is a valuable contribution.  The analysis of activation patterns (specifically the consistency of activation differences between few-shot and zero-shot prompts across tasks) is an interesting empirical finding, supporting the feasibility of latent space steering for cross-task transfer. The influential and diverse subset selection strategy is also a good, potentially reusable strategy.

**Significance:** The significance of the work lies in its ability to improve cross-task transfer, especially in data-scarce situations. The proposed method demonstrates that effective transfer can be achieved through latent space manipulation. The advantages of the method in terms of scalability and computational efficiency are also significant, particularly given the increasing size and complexity of LLMs. By avoiding parameter updates, CAST can be applied efficiently. The empirical results are strong, demonstrating improvements over existing baselines in a variety of transfer scenarios.

**Strengths:**

*   The analysis of activation patterns is a novel empirical contribution that informs the design of CAST.
*   The CAST framework is well-motivated and provides a clear and efficient approach to cross-task transfer.
*   The method shows good performance across different domains, languages, and LLMs, indicating generalizability.
*   The focus on scalability and efficiency is particularly relevant for large language models.
*   Ablation studies effectively demonstrate the importance of the key components of CAST.
*   The inclusion of a case study provides some insight into the method's inner workings.

**Weaknesses:**

*   The reliance on internal activations makes the method inapplicable to closed-source LLMs (as the authors themselves acknowledge). This limits the method's widespread adoption and applicability.
*   While demonstrating effectiveness, the paper does not delve deeply into *why* these particular patterns of activation steering work so well, hindering a broader theoretical understanding. More analysis of the semantic changes induced by the activation injection could be helpful.
*   The hyperparameter tuning analysis demonstrates only a minor impact on overall performance, making unclear which are the optimal values for various datasets.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence future research in cross-task transfer and latent space manipulation in LLMs. The idea of using activation steering to adapt models to new tasks without parameter updates could inspire new approaches. The finding that activation differences are consistent across tasks could be a valuable starting point for other studies on latent space representations. Overall, it presents a viable and useful new approach to this very challenging problem of cross-task transfer.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:**
The paper presents a novel and promising approach to cross-task transfer learning with strong empirical results. The concept of activation steering provides a new perspective on adaptation, offering a more efficient and scalable alternative to traditional methods. However, the dependence on internal activations (a limitation), combined with the absence of a thorough analysis of why specific activation patterns are effective (lack of theoretical depth), prevents this work from scoring higher. It is also unclear how some hyper parameters affect the performance for various datasets. Nevertheless, the method's innovative design, practical benefits, and consistent outperformance of strong baselines makes it a valuable contribution to the field, warranting a score of 7.5.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### HATS: Hindi Analogy Test Set for Evaluating Reasoning in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.13238v1)
- **Authors**: Ashray Gupta, Rohan Joseph, Sunny Rai
- **Abstract**: Analogies test a model's ability to infer implicit relationships between concepts, making them a key benchmark for evaluating reasoning capabilities. While large language models (LLMs) are widely evaluated for reasoning in English, their abilities in Indic languages remain understudied, limiting our understanding of whether these models generalize across languages. To address this gap, we introduce a new Hindi Analogy Test Set (HATS), comprising 405 multiple-choice questions sourced from Indian government exams. We benchmark state-of-the-art multilingual LLMs using various prompting strategies and introduce a grounded Chain of Thought approach that leverages cognitive theories of analogical reasoning. This approach improves model performance on Hindi analogy questions. Our experiments show that models perform best with English prompts, irrespective of the prompting strategy. Our test set addresses the lack of a critical resource to evaluate LLM reasoning capabilities in Hindi.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the HATS paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces HATS, a new Hindi Analogy Test Set, comprising 405 multiple-choice questions sourced from Indian government exams, designed to evaluate the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs). The authors benchmarked state-of-the-art multilingual LLMs using various prompting strategies, including a novel "grounded Chain of Thought" approach that leverages cognitive theories of analogical reasoning. Their experiments demonstrated that models generally perform best with English prompts, highlighting a gap in the ability of these models to effectively reason in Hindi. The resource aims to address the lack of available benchmarks for evaluating reasoning capabilities in Indic languages.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The primary novelty of this paper lies in the creation of the HATS dataset itself. While analogy tasks are not new, and chain-of-thought prompting is established, the application to Hindi, using in-situ data (i.e., questions from actual exams), adds a valuable, culturally relevant dimension. The grounded Chain of Thought (CoT) prompting is an incremental improvement, but not a major breakthrough. Prior work has used cognitive theory to inform prompt design in other domains; the adaptation to Hindi analogies is well-motivated but not inherently groundbreaking.  Much of the underlying methods are borrowed and applied rather than fundamentally new.

**Significance:**

The significance stems from addressing a known gap: the lack of adequate benchmarks for evaluating LLMs in Indic languages.  LLM research often focuses on English and other high-resource languages, potentially leading to biased or incomplete assessments of model capabilities. HATS provides a much-needed resource for evaluating LLMs in a low-resource language, contributing to fairer and more representative evaluations. It offers insights into the generalizability of reasoning skills across languages, particularly in semantic domains specific to the Indian context. The exploration of prompting strategies, including grounded CoT, contributes to the broader understanding of how to elicit better reasoning performance from LLMs, albeit with limited success within this work.

**Strengths:**

*   **Valuable Resource Creation:** The creation and public release of the HATS dataset is the main strength. The in-situ data ensures ecological validity.
*   **Addressing a Gap:** The paper directly tackles the under-representation of Indic languages in LLM benchmarking.
*   **Clear Methodology:** The experimental setup and prompting strategies are clearly described, allowing for reproducibility and further research.
*   **Motivated Prompting:** The grounded CoT approach is logically motivated by cognitive theories of analogy.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental CoT Contribution:** The grounded CoT approach shows only modest improvements. The fact that English prompts consistently outperform Hindi suggests the LLMs struggle with understanding and processing Hindi even with the structured reasoning prompt. This calls into question the effectiveness of this grounded CoT *in Hindi*.
*   **Limited Model Exploration:** While three multilingual LLMs were assessed, the range could be expanded.  Also, only smaller (8-9B) model sizes were used.
*   **Suboptimal Performance:** Overall accuracy remains relatively low, indicating significant room for improvement and potentially highlighting the limitations of current LLMs in handling semantic nuances in Hindi. This casts some doubt on the claim that this approach improves model performance as the increases may be statistically insignificant.
* **Lack of Error Analysis:** The paper lacks a detailed error analysis to categorize the specific types of errors the LLMs are making. This would provide more targeted insights for future research.
* The paper lacks a discussion of potential bias in the exams used as sources of the dataset and how the authors attempted to mitigate those biases.

**Potential Influence:**

HATS has the potential to become a standard benchmark for evaluating LLM reasoning in Hindi. It can stimulate further research in developing more effective prompting strategies, training methods, and model architectures specifically tailored for Indic languages. The dataset can also be used to train and fine-tune models for improved performance in Hindi reasoning tasks. The focus on cognitive theory-informed prompting could encourage similar approaches in other low-resource languages.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper's strengths lie in addressing a clear need and contributing a new resource to the field, the limitations in the proposed prompting techniques and overall model performance temper the significance. The impact is also somewhat bounded by the limited scope (Hindi analogies) and the incremental nature of the methodological contribution. The creation of a new resource is a valuable addition, and the attempt to use more sophisticated prompts is promising, but the relatively poor results detract from the impact. Therefore:

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Automating Steering for Safe Multimodal Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.13255v1)
- **Authors**: Lyucheng Wu, Mengru Wang, Ziwen Xu, Tri Cao, Nay Oo, Bryan Hooi, Shumin Deng
- **Abstract**: Recent progress in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) has unlocked powerful cross-modal reasoning abilities, but also raised new safety concerns, particularly when faced with adversarial multimodal inputs. To improve the safety of MLLMs during inference, we introduce a modular and adaptive inference-time intervention technology, AutoSteer, without requiring any fine-tuning of the underlying model. AutoSteer incorporates three core components: (1) a novel Safety Awareness Score (SAS) that automatically identifies the most safety-relevant distinctions among the model's internal layers; (2) an adaptive safety prober trained to estimate the likelihood of toxic outputs from intermediate representations; and (3) a lightweight Refusal Head that selectively intervenes to modulate generation when safety risks are detected. Experiments on LLaVA-OV and Chameleon across diverse safety-critical benchmarks demonstrate that AutoSteer significantly reduces the Attack Success Rate (ASR) for textual, visual, and cross-modal threats, while maintaining general abilities. These findings position AutoSteer as a practical, interpretable, and effective framework for safer deployment of multimodal AI systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Automating Steering for Safe Multimodal Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AutoSteer, a novel, automated, and adaptive inference-time intervention framework for improving the safety of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) without retraining. AutoSteer comprises three key components: a Safety Awareness Score (SAS) for identifying safety-relevant model layers, an adaptive safety prober for estimating toxicity, and a Refusal Head for selective intervention. Experiments across diverse safety-critical benchmarks using LLaVA-OV and Chameleon demonstrate significant reductions in Attack Success Rate (ASR) for textual, visual, and cross-modal threats, while preserving general capabilities. AutoSteer offers a practical, interpretable, and effective framework for safer MLLM deployment.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a crucial and timely issue: the safety concerns associated with increasingly powerful MLLMs. MLLMs, while offering significant capabilities in cross-modal reasoning, also present increased vulnerabilities to adversarial inputs and the generation of harmful content.  The core idea of inference-time intervention is not entirely new (LM-Steer, for example), but AutoSteer contributes several novel aspects:

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the automated and adaptive nature of the framework.
    *   The **SAS score** to automatically identify the most safety-relevant layers is a significant improvement over manual layer selection. It brings adaptability to different models without human intervention.
    *   The integration of a safety prober and a conditional Refusal Head allows for more fine-grained control than simply applying a global steering vector. This adaptability is crucial for maintaining general performance.
*   **Significance:**  The significance of the paper stems from its practicality and effectiveness.  It's a modular framework that can be applied to a variety of MLLMs *without* requiring fine-tuning of the underlying model. This is a major advantage, as fine-tuning can be computationally expensive and potentially degrade other capabilities. The experimental results demonstrate a substantial reduction in ASR across various toxicity sources, which is a strong indication of its practical impact. The detailed analysis of various components is also important.

**Strengths:**

*   Clear problem definition and motivation.
*   Well-defined framework with novel components (SAS, adaptive prober, Refusal Head).
*   Comprehensive experimental evaluation across diverse benchmarks and MLLMs.
*   Detailed analysis of the interpretability, stability, and robustness of the proposed mechanisms.
*   Thorough discussion of the limitations and potential future directions.
*   The approach is practical, as it's applicable at inference time and doesn't require model retraining.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dependency on prober quality:** While the SAS automates layer selection, the effectiveness of AutoSteer is still heavily dependent on the *quality* and diversity of the training data used to create the safety prober. The authors acknowledge this limitation and highlight the potential for the prober to generalize poorly to out-of-distribution harmful inputs. The quality of the safety-contrastive pairs generated is key.
*   **Control of Steering Intensity:** While steering intensity 'e' is explored, further work is needed in optimizing this parameter dynamically depending on the type and level of toxicity.
*   **Limited models tested:** Although the paper considers two representative MLLMs (LLaVA-OV and Chameleon), further validation on a wider range of architectures, particularly larger and more recent models, is necessary to assess the generalizability of AutoSteer.
*   **Limited focus on toxicity nuance:** The paper focuses primarily on reducing ASR, but further exploration of the *type* of errors made by the system (e.g., harmful but subtly coded content vs. explicit and easily detectable content) would strengthen the analysis.

**Potential Influence:**

AutoSteer has the potential to influence the field by providing a practical and effective approach to enhancing MLLM safety. The SAS concept is particularly valuable and could inspire further research into methods for automatically identifying safety-relevant features in neural networks. The modularity of the framework makes it adaptable to future advancements in MLLM architectures and safety techniques.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a significant and novel contribution to the field of MLLM safety. AutoSteer addresses a pressing concern with a practical, automated, and adaptive framework. While the dependence on prober quality and the relatively limited set of MLLMs tested represent limitations, the strengths of the approach, particularly the SAS score and the inference-time applicability, outweigh these weaknesses. The potential influence on future research in MLLM safety and the development of more robust safety mechanisms justifies a score of 8.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Efficient Adaptation of Pre-trained Vision Transformer underpinned by Approximately Orthogonal Fine-Tuning Strategy
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.13260v1)
- **Authors**: Yiting Yang, Hao Luo, Yuan Sun, Qingsen Yan, Haokui Zhang, Wei Dong, Guoqing Wang, Peng Wang, Yang Yang, Hengtao Shen
- **Abstract**: A prevalent approach in Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) of pre-trained Vision Transformers (ViT) involves freezing the majority of the backbone parameters and solely learning low-rank adaptation weight matrices to accommodate downstream tasks. These low-rank matrices are commonly derived through the multiplication structure of down-projection and up-projection matrices, exemplified by methods such as LoRA and Adapter. In this work, we observe an approximate orthogonality among any two row or column vectors within any weight matrix of the backbone parameters; however, this property is absent in the vectors of the down/up-projection matrices. Approximate orthogonality implies a reduction in the upper bound of the model's generalization error, signifying that the model possesses enhanced generalization capability. If the fine-tuned down/up-projection matrices were to exhibit this same property as the pre-trained backbone matrices, could the generalization capability of fine-tuned ViTs be further augmented? To address this question, we propose an Approximately Orthogonal Fine-Tuning (AOFT) strategy for representing the low-rank weight matrices. This strategy employs a single learnable vector to generate a set of approximately orthogonal vectors, which form the down/up-projection matrices, thereby aligning the properties of these matrices with those of the backbone. Extensive experimental results demonstrate that our method achieves competitive performance across a range of downstream image classification tasks, confirming the efficacy of the enhanced generalization capability embedded in the down/up-projection matrices.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a new Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) method for Vision Transformers (ViTs) called Approximately Orthogonal Fine-Tuning (AOFT).  The central idea revolves around the observation that weight matrices in pre-trained ViTs exhibit approximate orthogonality. In contrast, common PEFT methods like LoRA and Adapters, which introduce down/up-projection matrices, lack this property. AOFT aims to align the properties of the fine-tuned down/up-projection matrices with the backbone by enforcing approximate orthogonality within them. This is achieved by generating the down/up-projection matrices from a single learnable vector, ensuring approximate orthogonality among their rows or columns. The authors hypothesize that this alignment enhances the generalization capability of the fine-tuned ViT. They evaluate AOFT by integrating it with existing PEFT techniques (LoRA, Adapter, and VPT) on several image classification datasets (FGVC and VTAB-1k), showing competitive performance and reduced parameter count.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of enforcing approximate orthogonality in the down/up-projection matrices to align with the pre-trained backbone's properties has some novelty. However, the core concept leverages the known benefits of orthogonal matrices which is not particularly innovative. The paper builds upon well-established PEFT methods (LoRA, Adapter, VPT), integrating AOFT as a component.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in the potential to improve generalization in PEFT methods by explicitly considering and preserving the orthogonal properties of pre-trained weights. Reduced parameter count while maintaining or improving performance is valuable. The experimental results, while promising, don't consistently show substantial improvements over existing methods.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The observation regarding approximate orthogonality in pre-trained ViT weights is insightful.
    *   The AOFT strategy is relatively simple to implement and integrate with other PEFT methods.
    *   The paper provides a theoretical justification (using Rademacher complexity) for why approximate orthogonality might improve generalization.
    *   Experiments on a range of datasets and with different PEFT methods demonstrate the broad applicability of AOFT.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The "approximate orthogonality" argument is slightly weakened by the fact that perfectly orthogonal matrices are often undesirable for representation learning because they constrain the learned features too much. The approximation aspect is likely key, but this is not discussed.
    *   The improvement compared to baselines is often marginal. While AOFT consistently improves upon baselines, the gains aren't large enough to be considered a significant breakthrough.
    *   The parameter reduction, while mentioned as a benefit, isn't a game-changer. Other compression and PEFT techniques already achieve significant parameter reductions.
    *   The paper could benefit from more in-depth ablation studies.  For instance, a direct comparison against a randomly initialized down/up projection matrices with orthogonality enforced might be valuable.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence the field by encouraging researchers to consider the properties of pre-trained weights when designing PEFT methods. However, given the incremental nature of the improvement, its long-term influence might be limited.

*   **Rigorousness:** The experimental section is adequately designed, including various state-of-the-art methods for performance comparison. The reported findings do hold.

**Score: 6**

**Justification:**

A score of 6 reflects the paper's solid contribution with a novel idea but also highlights its limitations. While the concept of preserving approximate orthogonality in down/up-projection matrices is interesting and the method is reasonably effective, the empirical gains are modest, and the theoretical justifications are somewhat standard. The innovation, while present, isn't revolutionary and the experimental results don't definitively establish AOFT as a superior PEFT method compared to existing techniques. The paper encourages a slightly different viewpoint in PEFT design, but doesn't drastically alter the landscape. The strengths and weaknesses are relatively balanced, leading to a mid-range score.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Overview of the TalentCLEF 2025: Skill and Job Title Intelligence for Human Capital Management
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.13275v1)
- **Authors**: Luis Gasco, Hermenegildo Fabregat, Laura García-Sardiña, Paula Estrella, Daniel Deniz, Alvaro Rodrigo, Rabih Zbib
- **Abstract**: Advances in natural language processing and large language models are driving a major transformation in Human Capital Management, with a growing interest in building smart systems based on language technologies for talent acquisition, upskilling strategies, and workforce planning. However, the adoption and progress of these technologies critically depend on the development of reliable and fair models, properly evaluated on public data and open benchmarks, which have so far been unavailable in this domain. To address this gap, we present TalentCLEF 2025, the first evaluation campaign focused on skill and job title intelligence. The lab consists of two tasks: Task A - Multilingual Job Title Matching, covering English, Spanish, German, and Chinese; and Task B - Job Title-Based Skill Prediction, in English. Both corpora were built from real job applications, carefully anonymized, and manually annotated to reflect the complexity and diversity of real-world labor market data, including linguistic variability and gender-marked expressions. The evaluations included monolingual and cross-lingual scenarios and covered the evaluation of gender bias. TalentCLEF attracted 76 registered teams with more than 280 submissions. Most systems relied on information retrieval techniques built with multilingual encoder-based models fine-tuned with contrastive learning, and several of them incorporated large language models for data augmentation or re-ranking. The results show that the training strategies have a larger effect than the size of the model alone. TalentCLEF provides the first public benchmark in this field and encourages the development of robust, fair, and transferable language technologies for the labor market.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents an overview of TalentCLEF 2025, the first evaluation campaign focused on skill and job title intelligence for Human Capital Management (HCM).  The campaign comprised two tasks: (A) Multilingual Job Title Matching (English, Spanish, German, Chinese) and (B) Job Title-Based Skill Prediction (English).  The datasets were built from anonymized real job applications and manually annotated. The evaluation included monolingual, cross-lingual scenarios, and gender bias analysis. The campaign attracted 76 registered teams with 280+ submissions. Most systems used information retrieval techniques based on fine-tuned multilingual encoder models, with some using large language models (LLMs) for data augmentation or re-ranking. The results indicate that training strategies have a larger effect than just the size of the model itself. TalentCLEF provides a public benchmark for HCM and promotes fair, robust, and transferable language technologies.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The most significant aspect of this paper is the **creation and release of a public benchmark** for skill and job title intelligence in the HCM domain.  Prior to this, research in the field relied heavily on proprietary datasets, hindering progress due to privacy constraints, lack of standardized evaluation metrics, and challenges in comparing different approaches.  The inclusion of multilingual and gender bias evaluation aspects is also noteworthy, reflecting important real-world considerations. While individual techniques used by participating teams (e.g., fine-tuning, contrastive learning) are well-established in NLP, their application and systematic evaluation in this specific HCM context is novel.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper stems from addressing a critical need within HCM: developing reliable, fair, and transferable language technologies.  As the paper argues, this is crucial for talent acquisition, upskilling strategies, and workforce planning in the rapidly changing labor market.  The benchmark provides a common ground for researchers to compare and improve their models, and the evaluation of gender bias encourages the development of fairer systems.  The detailed analysis of participant methodologies provides useful insights for other researchers and practitioners. The availability of the datasets, along with the evaluation script, is important to the advancement of the field.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Creation of a Public Benchmark:**  This is the main strength and key contribution. It allows for open research and replicable results.
    *   **Real-World Data:**  Using real job application data increases the practical relevance of the benchmark.
    *   **Multilingual and Bias Considerations:** Addressing these key challenges in HCM is essential.
    *   **Comprehensive Overview:** The paper provides a thorough overview of the tasks, datasets, evaluation metrics, and participant methodologies.
    *   **Analysis of Results:** The paper presents an analysis of the outcomes and insights gained, along with a brief discussion of observations across system approaches.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Methodologies:** Most of the methodologies used by participants were not entirely novel in and of themselves, but rather adapted existing NLP techniques.
    *   **Limited Results:** The paper primarily summarizes the tasks and results. A more in-depth analysis of individual system performance or specific challenges faced by participants would have been valuable.
    *   **Lack of comparative Analysis**: The paper presents general findings but lacks some in-depth quantitative comparative analyses of different approaches.

*   **Impact and Potential Influence:** This paper is likely to have a considerable impact on the HCM field. The benchmark it introduces is a valuable resource for researchers and practitioners. It will encourage further research, the development of new methodologies, and the creation of fairer and more effective HCM systems. The identified best practices and insights into the performance of different models will guide future work.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the above evaluation, I would assign this paper a score of **8**. While the individual techniques used by the participants are not groundbreaking, the **creation of a much-needed public benchmark in the HCM domain, alongside with multilingual and bias considerations, makes this paper a significant contribution**. It addresses a clear gap in the field and is likely to have a lasting impact. The limitations of the work lie in a lack of more detailed analyses of methods, and some of the challenges faced by participants which prevents it from reaching a higher score.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### DiffClean: Diffusion-based Makeup Removal for Accurate Age Estimation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.13292v1)
- **Authors**: Ekta Balkrishna Gavas, Chinmay Hegde, Nasir Memon, Sudipta Banerjee
- **Abstract**: Accurate age verification can protect underage users from unauthorized access to online platforms and e-commerce sites that provide age-restricted services. However, accurate age estimation can be confounded by several factors, including facial makeup that can induce changes to alter perceived identity and age to fool both humans and machines. In this work, we propose DiffClean which erases makeup traces using a text-guided diffusion model to defend against makeup attacks. DiffClean improves age estimation (minor vs. adult accuracy by 4.8%) and face verification (TMR by 8.9% at FMR=0.01%) over competing baselines on digitally simulated and real makeup images.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DIFFCLEAN, a novel framework for removing makeup from face images using a text-guided diffusion model. The primary motivation is to improve the accuracy of age estimation and face verification, particularly in scenarios where makeup confounds these tasks, potentially impacting online safety for minors. DIFFCLEAN utilizes a combination of CLIP loss (for makeup detection), perceptual loss (for image fidelity), biometric loss (for identity preservation), and age loss (for restoring age cues). The authors demonstrate that DIFFCLEAN improves age estimation accuracy and face verification performance compared to existing baselines on both digitally simulated and real-world makeup images.  They also emphasized the ethical consideration of the work since it involves minors.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates novelty in several aspects:
    *   **Text-Guided Makeup Removal:**  The application of a text-guided diffusion model for makeup removal, specifically aimed at improving age estimation and face verification, is a relatively new approach. While diffusion models for image editing and style transfer are known, the specific formulation and objective (age estimation robustness) are novel.
    *   **Loss Function Combination:** The combination of CLIP, perceptual, biometric, and age losses is tailored to the specific task of makeup removal with the goal of age cue and identity restoration, distinguishing it from more generic image editing tasks.
    *   **Focus on Minor Age Groups:** Addressing the impact of makeup on age estimation for minors is a focused contribution, aligning with online safety concerns. Much prior work focuses on adults.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a practically relevant problem with potential real-world impact:
    *   **Improved Age Verification:**  Accurate age verification is crucial for protecting minors from harmful content and interactions online. Improving the robustness of age estimation against makeup manipulation is a significant contribution.
    *   **Enhanced Face Verification:** Makeup can also impede face verification systems. DIFFCLEAN's ability to remove makeup and restore identity cues is valuable for biometric authentication applications.
    *   **Ethical Considerations:** By explicitly acknowledging and addressing the ethical implications of the technology (potential misuse for malicious image editing) and focusing on responsible usage (assisting facial analytics), the authors contribute to the responsible development of AI.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper includes quantitative results on both synthetic and real-world datasets, demonstrating improved performance compared to baselines.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** The inclusion of ablation studies (loss component analysis) strengthens the argument for the design choices made in DIFFCLEAN.
    *   **Qualitative Results:**  The qualitative results (visual examples) provide insights into the method's ability to remove makeup effectively while preserving identity.
    *   **Responsible AI discussion:** Addresses and discusses the ethical ramifications of the work

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependency on Pre-trained Models:**  The framework relies on pre-trained models like SSRNet and EleGANt. The overall performance is therefore limited by the capabilities of those individual modules. While the authors fine-tune SSRNet, there's always a chance for error propagation.
    *   **Limited Real-World Datasets:**  While real-world datasets are used, they can be improved in size and diversity. Demonstrating robustness across a wider range of ethnicities, makeup styles, and lighting conditions would further validate the approach.
    *   **Computational Cost:** The paper states a 2-second inference time, but it might be useful to benchmark the approach for applications with more stringent real-time requirements. Diffusion models are often computationally demanding.
    *   **Specific choice of baselines:** More recent work exists that could be included for comparison to demonstrate the advantages of the paper's approach.
    *   **Figure clarity could be improved.** Some figures could be improved in terms of the resolution of images and color contrast, and labels on certain ROC curves.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research in:
    *   **Makeup-invariant facial analysis:**  It can spur further development of techniques for improving the robustness of face recognition, age estimation, and other facial analysis tasks in the presence of makeup.
    *   **Diffusion-based image editing:** It can inspire new applications of diffusion models for targeted image manipulations with specific goals, such as restoring certain attributes or cues.
    *   **Responsible AI for minors' safety:** It can encourage more researchers to consider the ethical implications and responsible use of AI technologies that affect vulnerable populations.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field. The application of a text-guided diffusion model for makeup removal with a focus on age estimation for minors is timely and addresses a practically relevant problem. The results are promising, and the inclusion of ablation studies and ethical considerations strengthens the work. However, the reliance on pre-trained models, limited diversity of real-world datasets, and potential computational costs prevent it from achieving a higher score. Including more recent approaches for comparison and an even more rigorous assessment of biases would also strengthen the study further.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### AbGen: Evaluating Large Language Models in Ablation Study Design and Evaluation for Scientific Research
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.13300v1)
- **Authors**: Yilun Zhao, Weiyuan Chen, Zhijian Xu, Manasi Patwardhan, Yixin Liu, Chengye Wang, Lovekesh Vig, Arman Cohan
- **Abstract**: We introduce AbGen, the first benchmark designed to evaluate the capabilities of LLMs in designing ablation studies for scientific research. AbGen consists of 1,500 expert-annotated examples derived from 807 NLP papers. In this benchmark, LLMs are tasked with generating detailed ablation study designs for a specified module or process based on the given research context. Our evaluation of leading LLMs, such as DeepSeek-R1-0528 and o4-mini, highlights a significant performance gap between these models and human experts in terms of the importance, faithfulness, and soundness of the ablation study designs. Moreover, we demonstrate that current automated evaluation methods are not reliable for our task, as they show a significant discrepancy when compared to human assessment. To better investigate this, we develop AbGen-Eval, a meta-evaluation benchmark designed to assess the reliability of commonly used automated evaluation systems in measuring LLM performance on our task. We investigate various LLM-as-Judge systems on AbGen-Eval, providing insights for future research on developing more effective and reliable LLM-based evaluation systems for complex scientific tasks.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "ABGEN: Evaluating Large Language Models in Ablation Study Design and Evaluation for Scientific Research."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ABGEN, a novel benchmark designed to assess the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to design ablation studies for scientific research, specifically within the field of Natural Language Processing (NLP). The benchmark consists of 1,500 expert-annotated examples extracted from 807 NLP papers. LLMs are tasked with generating detailed ablation study designs for specific modules or processes based on provided research contexts.  The authors evaluate various leading LLMs, revealing a significant performance gap compared to human experts in terms of importance, faithfulness, and soundness of the designed ablation studies. The paper also highlights the unreliability of current automated evaluation methods for this task and introduces ABGEN-EVAL, a meta-evaluation benchmark, to investigate and improve LLM-based evaluation systems. User studies demonstrate the potential of LLMs in assisting human researchers, but also highlight the limitations of current models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The creation of ABGEN is a significant contribution. It's the first dedicated benchmark for evaluating LLMs in the specific, and complex, task of ablation study design. Previous benchmarks focused on more general scientific tasks, or specific ones like review generation, making ABGEN a unique resource. ABGEN-EVAL is also a valuable resource as it can be used to study the reliability of automated evaluations of LLMs for complex tasks.

*   **Significance:** Ablation study design is a critical part of scientific research. Automating or augmenting this process with LLMs has the potential to accelerate scientific progress. By providing a structured way to evaluate and compare LLMs, ABGEN facilitates progress in this area.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined task:** The paper clearly defines the ablation study design task and provides a formal formulation.
    *   **Comprehensive dataset:** ABGEN consists of a large and diverse dataset, meticulously curated and validated by NLP experts. The careful annotation process and validation enhance the reliability of the benchmark.
    *   **Rigorous evaluation:** The paper employs both human and automated evaluation methods, providing a comprehensive assessment of LLM performance. The inclusion of a meta-evaluation benchmark (ABGEN-EVAL) adds another layer of rigor.
    *   **Error analysis:** The detailed error analysis provides valuable insights into the limitations of current LLMs and suggests directions for future research.
    *   **User Studies:** By incorporating user studies they were able to showcase the capabilities and limitations of LLMs when working with researchers in real-world scenarios.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **NLP Focus:** The benchmark is limited to the NLP domain. While this allows for expert annotation, it restricts the generalizability of the findings to other scientific fields. The authors acknowledge this, but the extent of the limitation should be considered.
    *   **Automated Evaluation:** The paper clearly demonstrates the weaknesses of current automated evaluation systems. While ABGEN-EVAL is a valuable step, reliance on LLM-as-judge methods remains problematic, and the paper doesn't offer a definitive solution.
    *   **Limited Scope of Experiments:** Although the study evaluates a diverse set of LLMs, it doesn't delve deeply into advanced prompting techniques or LLM-agent based systems for improving performance.

*   **Impact:**  The paper is likely to have a significant impact on the field by:

    *   Providing a valuable benchmark for evaluating and comparing LLMs in ablation study design.
    *   Stimulating research into more effective and reliable automated evaluation methods for complex scientific tasks.
    *   Guiding the development of LLMs that can effectively assist scientists in their research workflows.
    *   Highlighting the areas where future progress is needed.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper is a valuable contribution that addresses an important problem. The benchmark and meta-evaluation benchmark it provides are substantial assets to the research community. While the NLP focus and the current limitations in automated evaluation methods are weaknesses, the strengths of the paper outweigh these limitations. The work is likely to have a tangible and positive influence on the application of LLMs in scientific research. Therefore, the paper warrants a high score.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### The Generative Energy Arena (GEA): Incorporating Energy Awareness in Large Language Model (LLM) Human Evaluations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.13302v1)
- **Authors**: Carlos Arriaga, Gonzalo Martínez, Eneko Sendin, Javier Conde, Pedro Reviriego
- **Abstract**: The evaluation of large language models is a complex task, in which several approaches have been proposed. The most common is the use of automated benchmarks in which LLMs have to answer multiple-choice questions of different topics. However, this method has certain limitations, being the most concerning, the poor correlation with the humans. An alternative approach, is to have humans evaluate the LLMs. This poses scalability issues as there is a large and growing number of models to evaluate making it impractical (and costly) to run traditional studies based on recruiting a number of evaluators and having them rank the responses of the models. An alternative approach is the use of public arenas, such as the popular LM arena, on which any user can freely evaluate models on any question and rank the responses of two models. The results are then elaborated into a model ranking. An increasingly important aspect of LLMs is their energy consumption and, therefore, evaluating how energy awareness influences the decisions of humans in selecting a model is of interest. In this paper, we present GEA, the Generative Energy Arena, an arena that incorporates information on the energy consumption of the model in the evaluation process. Preliminary results obtained with GEA are also presented, showing that for most questions, when users are aware of the energy consumption, they favor smaller and more energy efficient models. This suggests that for most user interactions, the extra cost and energy incurred by the more complex and top-performing models do not provide an increase in the perceived quality of the responses that justifies their use.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the Generative Energy Arena (GEA), a platform designed to incorporate energy awareness into human evaluations of large language models (LLMs).  The GEA presents users with the outputs of two LLMs and asks them to select the better response. After an initial selection, if the chosen response comes from the more energy-intensive model, the user is prompted to reconsider their choice in light of the energy consumption difference. The paper presents preliminary results showing that users often switch their preference to the more energy-efficient model when made aware of the energy cost. The authors argue that this suggests energy awareness is an important factor in human evaluation and should be considered in future LLM development and evaluation methodologies.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Addresses an Important and Timely Issue:** The paper tackles the growing concern of energy consumption by LLMs, a crucial factor for sustainable AI development. Integrating this concern into human evaluation is a valuable step.
*   **Novel Arena Design:** The GEA design, particularly the two-step evaluation process, is a clever way to introduce energy awareness without directly biasing users upfront.
*   **Empirical Evidence:** The preliminary results provide empirical support for the idea that energy awareness influences user preferences, showing a measurable shift in votes towards energy-efficient models.
*   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the GEA design, methodology, and findings.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope and Scale:** The study is based on a relatively small sample of questions and users, primarily from a single MOOC. This limits the generalizability of the findings.
*   **Limited LLM Selection:**  Focusing primarily on GPT models (and variants), Claude and Llama leaves out a wide range of other LLMs and architectures. The conclusions might not hold for all LLMs.
*   **Spanish Language Bias:** The analysis is conducted primarily in Spanish, which could introduce cultural or linguistic biases.
*   **Relative Energy Comparison:** While using relative energy comparisons is practical, it does not provide a sense of the *magnitude* of the energy difference, potentially diminishing the effect of energy awareness. It also does not compare across families, i.e., a GPT mini version and a Claude model.
*   **Homogeneous User Profile:** The user base consists primarily of MOOC students, who may have specific motivations or knowledge levels that are not representative of the general population.
*   **Lack of Analysis of Question Type:** The analysis does not deeply explore how the *type* of question asked influences the impact of energy awareness. Some questions might inherently favor performance over efficiency.
*   **The GEA design is fairly basic and lacks the sophistication of existing platforms.** The comparison choices are all the same models, and there is a risk that the LLMs can be vote brigaded.
*   **The model comparison approach favors the approach of minimizing model size within the same family, but disregards the question as to whether larger models might be needed if there is too much data leakage across families.**

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper's primary novelty lies in the *design* of the GEA as a platform to systematically study the impact of energy awareness on human evaluation of LLMs. While the idea of considering energy consumption in AI is not new, the GEA offers a concrete mechanism for doing so in the context of subjective human judgments. The significance is moderate; it opens up a pathway to incorporate energy considerations in LLM assessments. However, the current implementation and analysis have substantial limitations.

The claim that "energy awareness should be incorporated in human evaluations" is reasonable but requires more robust evidence and a deeper understanding of the nuances involved. The finding that users *tend* to favor more energy-efficient models is interesting, but it's unclear how this preference translates into real-world usage or strategic decisions about model deployment. Further analysis is needed to address the limitations discussed and better understand the trade-offs between energy consumption and performance.
